They want to make everyone forget what I have done for this family, just erase me completely. \n\nAnd I have done so much! I mean, I used to wash the altar linens myself, by hand. You have to be so careful with those, you can't just throw them in the washing machine with bleach like Helen does. Helen ruined her husband's good work shirts doing that. He was a mechanic down at the garage on Elm Street, the one that closed down last summer so they could put in that new fancy coffee shop. I don't know why we need another coffee shop in this town, they charge five dollars for a plain black cup of coffee now, and it always tastes burnt. My husband bought a cup there once and he couldn't even finish it, had to pour it right down the sink... \n\nBut no, doctor, I'm not just making this up. I know exactly what my in-laws are doing with that committee.\n\nDoctor: How do you know for certain that your in-laws are using the altar and the parish committee against you?\n\nPatient: How do I know? Well, it's plain as day if you just open your eyes. Last Sunday, they changed the altar cloths to the green ones a whole week early! I used to be in charge of the liturgical colors, you know, so I know exactly what they are doing. My sister-in-law Joanne is on that parish committee now, and she's the one directing them to change the altar just to spite me. It's their way of pushing me out, making sure the whole congregation and the family forget every single thing I've done for them over the years. \n\nGreen is for Ordinary Time in the church calendar, you know. Though I suppose time just flies by anyway. I had to get a new watch battery at the jeweler downtown on Tuesday, the little shop right next to the post office. The line at the post office was completely out the door because they only ever have one window open. I just don't understand why they can't hire more people. My nephew David was looking for a job, but he only wants to work with computers. Kids today just stare at screens all day, they don't know the value of hard work, not like how I worked for this family... but yes, that's how I know. Joanne and the committee are plotting the whole thing right in front of everyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others, outside of your family and the parish committee, would agree that your in-laws are plotting against you?\n\nPatient: Would others agree? Well, anyone paying attention would! That's exactly why I came in here today, to tell someone what's really happening. Because I finally realized they are not just arguing with me. It's not just normal family bickering. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI was just trying to explain this to my neighbor, Betty. She came over to borrow my garden shears because her hydrangeas are completely out of control. You have to prune them back in late winter, you know, or they just take over the whole front walkway. I told her she needs to use aluminum sulfate if she wants the blooms to stay blue, because the soil around here is much too alkaline. My husband used to test the dirt every spring with those little paper strips. He bought a whole box of them at the local hardware store before it closed down and turned into that awful giant warehouse place out on the highway. I can't stand going in there. The ceilings are too high and those fluorescent lights just buzz and buzz, it gives me a terrible migraine. And you can walk down ten aisles and never see a single employee to ask where the furnace filters are... but yes, Betty would agree if she just looked at the facts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is following you or watching you?\n\nPatient: Following me? Well, I don't know about following me down the street, but they are definitely watching me. My in-laws, I mean. They have to be watching me to know exactly how to use the altar and the parish committee to push me out. They are coordinating the whole thing just to make everyone forget what I have done for this family, and they couldn't do that if they weren't keeping tabs on me.\n\nI used to keep tabs on the neighborhood watch, you know. Back when we had a proper neighborhood watch. We'd take turns walking the block with flashlights. We had these heavy metal flashlights, the kind that take those big D batteries. You go through so many batteries, and they're so expensive now. I went to the pharmacy to get some last week, and they had them locked up behind this plastic case! You have to press a button and wait for someone to come unlock it. I stood there for twenty minutes. The girl who finally came over looked like she was barely out of middle school. She had bright pink hair, just bright neon pink, right down to her shoulders. I don't understand how jobs let people look like that nowadays. When I worked the checkout at the grocery store—before they put in all those self-checkout machines where you have to scan your own apples—we had a strict dress code. White collared shirts, navy skirts. Very professional. But anyway... yes, they are watching me, making sure their little plot with the committee works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would actually be able to control the parish committee and the altar in the way you've described?\n\nPatient: Control the committee? Of course they can! That's exactly what I've been trying to tell you. I came in here specifically because I finally realized it wasn't just normal arguing anymore. They are absolutely using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and erase every single thing I've done for this family. Joanne has everyone on that committee wrapped around her little finger. \n\nShe always did know how to talk people into things. Like that time she convinced my brother to buy that awful timeshare in Florida. It was somewhere near Orlando, I think, but not near the parks. They had to sit through a three-hour presentation just to get free breakfast coupons. I told him it was a scam, but he wouldn't listen. He never listens. He bought a used car once from a man in a parking lot, just handed over cash! The transmission blew out two weeks later on the interstate. They had to get it towed all the way back to town, and you know how much tow trucks charge these days. It's highway robbery. The driver was a nice young man, though, helped him get his golf clubs out of the trunk. My brother loves golf, but he has a terrible swing. He slices it every time... but yes, Joanne is controlling the whole committee, there's no doubt in my mind about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the changes in the altar cloths or the parish committee's actions are specifically about you, or could they be about something else entirely?\n\nPatient: About me? Well, yes, of course it's about me! It couldn't possibly be about anything else. I told you, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee specifically to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI've done so much, you know. I organized the church bake sale for fifteen years straight. Fifteen years! I used to make these lemon bars from scratch. Not from a box, mind you. You have to use fresh lemons, Meyer lemons if you can find them, and you grate the zest right into the sugar before you mix the batter. It releases the oils, makes it so much more flavorful. My mother taught me that. She had a lemon tree in her backyard when I was growing up in California. Oh, the weather there was just perfect. Never too humid. I can't stand the humidity here, it makes my joints ache so terribly. I bought this special copper bracelet that's supposed to help with the arthritis, but I think it's turning my wrist a little green. I read in a magazine in the dentist's waiting room that you can paint clear nail polish on the inside of the jewelry to stop that from happening. The dentist was running forty-five minutes late that day, too. Just left me sitting there flipping through old copies of Better Homes and Gardens... but yes, the altar cloths, the committee, it's all about me. They just want me gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of your in-laws' actions and the parish committee's decisions might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No, no, I don't think it's incorrect at all. I know what I see. I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nMy husband's family has always been like this, you know, holding grudges over the silliest things. Like at Thanksgiving back in '98, I brought a sweet potato casserole instead of the green bean one because green beans were out of season and looking terribly limp at the grocer. You know how they get those brown spots and go all rubbery? You just can't serve that to guests. Anyway, Joanne wouldn't speak to me for the rest of the meal. She just sat there chewing her turkey—which was terribly dry, by the way, she never bastes it enough. You have to baste a turkey every forty-five minutes or it turns to sawdust in your mouth. I use a little chicken broth and melted butter for mine. I used to have this wonderful baster with a little flavor injector needle, but the bulb cracked. They just don't make rubber like they used to. Everything cracks or melts in the dishwasher now. I bought some plastic storage containers last month and they warped on the top rack! The lids don't snap on anymore, so now I have to use aluminum foil, which is so wasteful. But no... no, I'm not wrong about this. I know exactly what they're doing with the committee.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would be able to successfully erase your contributions to the family from everyone's memory?\n\nPatient: Erase my contributions? Yes, absolutely. That's the whole point of their plan. I came in here because it finally dawned on me—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, they were actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd I've kept this family together, let me tell you. When my father-in-law passed, I was the one who sorted through all his things in the attic. Dust everywhere. I found these beautiful old glass Christmas ornaments, the fragile kind from Germany. They were wrapped in newspaper from 1964. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now all the ornaments at the store are made of cheap plastic and covered in glitter that gets all over the rug. I vacuumed for an hour last Christmas just trying to get the glitter out of the fibers. I have one of those vacuums with the clear canister so you can see all the dirt spinning around. It's very satisfying, really, but it's so heavy to drag up the stairs. My knees aren't what they used to be. The doctor told me to take glucosamine, but the pills are the size of horse tranquilizers. I have to break them in half just to swallow them without choking. I choked on a piece of hard candy once when I was ten, a butterscotch disc. Terrifying. My uncle had to do the Heimlich maneuver on me... but yes, they are definitely trying to erase me. They want it to be like I was never here at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there could be any other explanation for the parish committee's actions that doesn't involve a plot against you?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? No, no, there isn't one. It's clear as day to me now. I mean, I told you, I came in because I finally realized it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, like normal families do. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd it's not just the church things, either. It's the little things. Like the way they look at me when I bring my famous macaroni salad to the summer picnics. I use the little shell pasta, not the elbows. The elbows get too mushy if you leave them in the dressing too long. You have to use a good quality mayonnaise, too, none of that low-fat nonsense. It separates in the heat. We had a picnic at the lake back in 2005, and it was so hot, I swear the pavement was melting. My husband—God rest his soul—got a terrible sunburn on his shoulders because he refused to wear the sunscreen I bought. It was that spray kind. I don't trust the sprays, honestly. You never know if you're getting an even coat. I always end up with these strange streaks of red and white, like a candy cane. Peppermint is my favorite flavor of tea, by the way. It settles the stomach. I have a cup every night before bed while I watch the evening news. The news is just so depressing lately, though, isn't it? All those terrible things happening in the world. But yes, there's no other explanation. They are plotting against me, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is out to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Harm me personally? Well, yes, in a way. I mean, it's like I've been saying, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. It's a spiritual harm, an erasure. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, they changed the linens last week. They used to use the beautiful Irish linen with the hand-embroidered crosses on the edges. My grandmother taught me how to embroider when I was a little girl. We would sit on the porch in the summer, and she had this big wooden hoop. I pricked my finger so many times on those tiny needles. You have to be so careful with blood on white fabric. Cold water and a little bit of salt, that's the trick. Never use hot water, it sets the stain right in. I ruined a perfectly good silk blouse that way once. I wore it to a wedding, my cousin Linda's wedding. She married a man who sold insurance, very boring fellow, but he had a nice smile. They had a chocolate fountain at the reception. Oh, what a mess that was! People were dropping strawberries into it and splashing chocolate all over the tablecloths. I just don't understand the appeal of those things. It seems terribly unsanitary, everyone dipping their sticks into the same flow of chocolate. But yes, pushing me out, erasing me... that's exactly the harm they are doing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your in-laws using the parish committee against you might be an exaggeration or misunderstanding of their actual actions?\n\nPatient: An exaggeration? No, I... I don't think you're hearing me. I wouldn't have come in here if it was just a misunderstanding. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. This isn't a petty squabble. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nThey think they're so clever, whispering after mass. Mass used to be so peaceful. Father Thomas had such a soothing voice, almost like a radio announcer. He used to do the late-night jazz hour on the college station before he went to seminary, you know. Jazz is too unpredictable for me, though. All those horns blaring out of nowhere. I prefer classical music. Mozart, Beethoven. My mother played the piano beautifully. She had this upright Baldwin in the parlor, polished mahogany. I tried to learn, but my fingers were always too stiff. The piano teacher, Mrs. Gable, she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint. Peppermint again, see? It really is everywhere. But she was very strict, used a metronome that ticked so loudly it gave me a headache. Tick, tick, tick... just like a clock counting down. And that's what they're doing, counting down the days until they can just scrub me right out of the family history. No, it's not an exaggeration at all. It's exactly what's happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not based on real events? Doctor, I feel like a broken record, but I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, the way families normally do. It is very real. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me completely out and make every single person forget what I have done for this family! 

Real events! I organized every family communion and baptism for thirty years! I made the little favor bags for the tables by hand. I used a lovely blue organza fabric for my nephew's christening. Organza is so tricky to sew, you know. It frays if you even look at it wrong. You really have to use pinking shears. My mother had a pair of heavy metal pinking shears that weighed a ton. She kept them in one of those Danish butter cookie tins. You know the ones, the dark blue tins? It is always such a terrible disappointment when you open one hoping for a cookie and it is just full of spools of thread and needles. I love those little pretzel-shaped cookies with the large sugar crystals on top. 

Although, sugar is getting so terribly expensive these days. I saw the price at the grocery store on Tuesday and nearly had a heart attack. I always do my shopping on Tuesdays because the store is quieter. The weekends are just a madhouse, people bumping into your cart and not even apologizing. Manners have just completely gone out the window in this country. People don't even hold the door for you anymore. But no, I am not making this up. The things they are doing at the church to get rid of me are very real.

It's their way of pushing me out, making sure the whole congregation and the family forget every single thing I've done for them over the years. \n\nGreen is for Ordinary Time in the church calendar, you know. The line at the post office was completely out the door because they only ever have one window open. I just don't understand why they can't hire more people. My nephew David was looking for a job, but he only wants to work with computers. Kids today just stare at screens all day, they don't know the value of hard work, not like how I worked for this family... but yes, that's how I know. Well, anyone paying attention would! That's exactly why I came in here today, to tell someone what's really happening. Because I finally realized they are not just arguing with me. It's not just normal family bickering. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI was just trying to explain this to my neighbor, Betty. She came over to borrow my garden shears because her hydrangeas are completely out of control. You have to prune them back in late winter, you know, or they just take over the whole front walkway. I told her she needs to use aluminum sulfate if she wants the blooms to stay blue, because the soil around here is much too alkaline. My husband used to test the dirt every spring with those little paper strips. He bought a whole box of them at the local hardware store before it closed down and turned into that awful giant warehouse place out on the highway. I can't stand going in there. The ceilings are too high and those fluorescent lights just buzz and buzz, it gives me a terrible migraine. Back when we had a proper neighborhood watch. We'd take turns walking the block with flashlights. We had these heavy metal flashlights, the kind that take those big D batteries. You go through so many batteries, and they're so expensive now. I went to the pharmacy to get some last week, and they had them locked up behind this plastic case! You have to press a button and wait for someone to come unlock it. I stood there for twenty minutes. The girl who finally came over looked like she was barely out of middle school. She had bright pink hair, just bright neon pink, right down to her shoulders. I don't understand how jobs let people look like that nowadays. When I worked the checkout at the grocery store—before they put in all those self-checkout machines where you have to scan your own apples—we had a strict dress code. White collared shirts, navy skirts. Very professional. Of course they can! That's exactly what I've been trying to tell you. I came in here specifically because I finally realized it wasn't just normal arguing anymore. They are absolutely using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and erase every single thing I've done for this family. Joanne has everyone on that committee wrapped around her little finger. \n\nShe always did know how to talk people into things. Like that time she convinced my brother to buy that awful timeshare in Florida. It was somewhere near Orlando, I think, but not near the parks. They had to sit through a three-hour presentation just to get free breakfast coupons. I told him it was a scam, but he wouldn't listen. He never listens. He bought a used car once from a man in a parking lot, just handed over cash! The transmission blew out two weeks later on the interstate. They had to get it towed all the way back to town, and you know how much tow trucks charge these days. It's highway robbery. The driver was a nice young man, though, helped him get his golf clubs out of the trunk. My brother loves golf, but he has a terrible swing. He slices it every time... but yes, Joanne is controlling the whole committee, there's no doubt in my mind about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the changes in the altar cloths or the parish committee's actions are specifically about you, or could they be about something else entirely?\n\nPatient: About me? Well, yes, of course it's about me! It couldn't possibly be about anything else. I told you, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee specifically to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI've done so much, you know. I organized the church bake sale for fifteen years straight. Fifteen years! I used to make these lemon bars from scratch. Not from a box, mind you. You have to use fresh lemons, Meyer lemons if you can find them, and you grate the zest right into the sugar before you mix the batter. It releases the oils, makes it so much more flavorful. My mother taught me that. She had a lemon tree in her backyard when I was growing up in California. Oh, the weather there was just perfect. Never too humid. I can't stand the humidity here, it makes my joints ache so terribly. I bought this special copper bracelet that's supposed to help with the arthritis, but I think it's turning my wrist a little green. I read in a magazine in the dentist's waiting room that you can paint clear nail polish on the inside of the jewelry to stop that from happening. The dentist was running forty-five minutes late that day, too. Just left me sitting there flipping through old copies of Better Homes and Gardens... but yes, the altar cloths, the committee, it's all about me. They just want me gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of your in-laws' actions and the parish committee's decisions might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No, no, I don't think it's incorrect at all. I know what I see. I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nMy husband's family has always been like this, you know, holding grudges over the silliest things. Like at Thanksgiving back in '98, I brought a sweet potato casserole instead of the green bean one because green beans were out of season and looking terribly limp at the grocer. You know how they get those brown spots and go all rubbery? You just can't serve that to guests. Anyway, Joanne wouldn't speak to me for the rest of the meal. She just sat there chewing her turkey—which was terribly dry, by the way, she never bastes it enough. You have to baste a turkey every forty-five minutes or it turns to sawdust in your mouth. I use a little chicken broth and melted butter for mine. I used to have this wonderful baster with a little flavor injector needle, but the bulb cracked. They just don't make rubber like they used to. Everything cracks or melts in the dishwasher now. I bought some plastic storage containers last month and they warped on the top rack! The lids don't snap on anymore, so now I have to use aluminum foil, which is so wasteful. But no... no, I'm not wrong about this. I know exactly what they're doing with the committee.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would be able to successfully erase your contributions to the family from everyone's memory?\n\nPatient: Erase my contributions? Yes, absolutely. That's the whole point of their plan. I came in here because it finally dawned on me—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, they were actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd I've kept this family together, let me tell you. When my father-in-law passed, I was the one who sorted through all his things in the attic. Dust everywhere. I found these beautiful old glass Christmas ornaments, the fragile kind from Germany. They were wrapped in newspaper from 1964. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now all the ornaments at the store are made of cheap plastic and covered in glitter that gets all over the rug. I vacuumed for an hour last Christmas just trying to get the glitter out of the fibers. I have one of those vacuums with the clear canister so you can see all the dirt spinning around. It's very satisfying, really, but it's so heavy to drag up the stairs. My knees aren't what they used to be. The doctor told me to take glucosamine, but the pills are the size of horse tranquilizers. I have to break them in half just to swallow them without choking. I choked on a piece of hard candy once when I was ten, a butterscotch disc. Terrifying. My uncle had to do the Heimlich maneuver on me... but yes, they are definitely trying to erase me. They want it to be like I was never here at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there could be any other explanation for the parish committee's actions that doesn't involve a plot against you?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? No, no, there isn't one. It's clear as day to me now. I mean, I told you, I came in because I finally realized it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, like normal families do. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd it's not just the church things, either. It's the little things. Like the way they look at me when I bring my famous macaroni salad to the summer picnics. I use the little shell pasta, not the elbows. The elbows get too mushy if you leave them in the dressing too long. You have to use a good quality mayonnaise, too, none of that low-fat nonsense. It separates in the heat. We had a picnic at the lake back in 2005, and it was so hot, I swear the pavement was melting. My husband—God rest his soul—got a terrible sunburn on his shoulders because he refused to wear the sunscreen I bought. It was that spray kind. I don't trust the sprays, honestly. You never know if you're getting an even coat. I always end up with these strange streaks of red and white, like a candy cane. Peppermint is my favorite flavor of tea, by the way. It settles the stomach. I have a cup every night before bed while I watch the evening news. The news is just so depressing lately, though, isn't it? All those terrible things happening in the world. But yes, there's no other explanation. They are plotting against me, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is out to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Harm me personally? Well, yes, in a way. I mean, it's like I've been saying, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. It's a spiritual harm, an erasure. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, they changed the linens last week. They used to use the beautiful Irish linen with the hand-embroidered crosses on the edges. My grandmother taught me how to embroider when I was a little girl. We would sit on the porch in the summer, and she had this big wooden hoop. I pricked my finger so many times on those tiny needles. You have to be so careful with blood on white fabric. Cold water and a little bit of salt, that's the trick. Never use hot water, it sets the stain right in. I ruined a perfectly good silk blouse that way once. I wore it to a wedding, my cousin Linda's wedding. She married a man who sold insurance, very boring fellow, but he had a nice smile. They had a chocolate fountain at the reception. Oh, what a mess that was! People were dropping strawberries into it and splashing chocolate all over the tablecloths. I just don't understand the appeal of those things. It seems terribly unsanitary, everyone dipping their sticks into the same flow of chocolate. But yes, pushing me out, erasing me... that's exactly the harm they are doing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your in-laws using the parish committee against you might be an exaggeration or misunderstanding of their actual actions?\n\nPatient: An exaggeration? No, I... I don't think you're hearing me. I wouldn't have come in here if it was just a misunderstanding. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. This isn't a petty squabble. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nThey think they're so clever, whispering after mass. Mass used to be so peaceful. Father Thomas had such a soothing voice, almost like a radio announcer. He used to do the late-night jazz hour on the college station before he went to seminary, you know. Jazz is too unpredictable for me, though. All those horns blaring out of nowhere. I prefer classical music. Mozart, Beethoven. My mother played the piano beautifully. She had this upright Baldwin in the parlor, polished mahogany. I tried to learn, but my fingers were always too stiff. The piano teacher, Mrs. Gable, she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint. Peppermint again, see? It really is everywhere. But she was very strict, used a metronome that ticked so loudly it gave me a headache. Tick, tick, tick... just like a clock counting down. And that's what they're doing, counting down the days until they can just scrub me right out of the family history. No, it's not an exaggeration at all. It's exactly what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your feelings of being pushed out by your in-laws are not based on real events?\n\nPatient: Not based on real events? Doctor, I feel like a broken record, but I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, the way families normally do. It is very real. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me completely out and make every single person forget what I have done for this family! \n\nReal events! I organized every family communion and baptism for thirty years! I made the little favor bags for the tables by hand. I used a lovely blue organza fabric for my nephew's christening. Organza is so tricky to sew, you know. It frays if you even look at it wrong. You really have to use pinking shears. My mother had a pair of heavy metal pinking shears that weighed a ton. She kept them in one of those Danish butter cookie tins. You know the ones, the dark blue tins? It is always such a terrible disappointment when you open one hoping for a cookie and it is just full of spools of thread and needles. I love those little pretzel-shaped cookies with the large sugar crystals on top. \n\nAlthough, sugar is getting so terribly expensive these days. I saw the price at the grocery store on Tuesday and nearly had a heart attack. I always do my shopping on Tuesdays because the store is quieter. The weekends are just a madhouse, people bumping into your cart and not even apologizing. Manners have just completely gone out the window in this country. People don't even hold the door for you anymore. But no, I am not making this up. The things they are doing at the church to get rid of me are very real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not intended to harm me? How can you even suggest that? I keep telling you, I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me, they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. It's completely deliberate!

Who puts celery in potato salad? It completely ruins the texture. You need a nice, soft bite, maybe a little sweet relish. My mother used to make her own relish from the cucumbers in our garden. We had such a big garden when I was growing up. Tomatoes, zucchini, green beans... the green beans would climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the garage. We had to paint that garage every three years because the paint would peel from the humidity. It was a pale yellow color, sort of like butter. Butter makes everything better, doesn't it? I always put a pat of butter on my oatmeal in the morning. It melts in so nicely. But yes, their intentions are clear as day. They want me gone and forgotten.

Back when we had a proper neighborhood watch. We'd take turns walking the block with flashlights. We had these heavy metal flashlights, the kind that take those big D batteries. You go through so many batteries, and they're so expensive now. I went to the pharmacy to get some last week, and they had them locked up behind this plastic case! You have to press a button and wait for someone to come unlock it. I stood there for twenty minutes. The girl who finally came over looked like she was barely out of middle school. She had bright pink hair, just bright neon pink, right down to her shoulders. I don't understand how jobs let people look like that nowadays. When I worked the checkout at the grocery store—before they put in all those self-checkout machines where you have to scan your own apples—we had a strict dress code. My mother taught me that. She had a lemon tree in her backyard when I was growing up in California. Oh, the weather there was just perfect. Never too humid. I can't stand the humidity here, it makes my joints ache so terribly. I bought this special copper bracelet that's supposed to help with the arthritis, but I think it's turning my wrist a little green. I read in a magazine in the dentist's waiting room that you can paint clear nail polish on the inside of the jewelry to stop that from happening. The dentist was running forty-five minutes late that day, too. Just left me sitting there flipping through old copies of Better Homes and Gardens... but yes, the altar cloths, the committee, it's all about me. They just want me gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of your in-laws' actions and the parish committee's decisions might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No, no, I don't think it's incorrect at all. I know what I see. I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nMy husband's family has always been like this, you know, holding grudges over the silliest things. Like at Thanksgiving back in '98, I brought a sweet potato casserole instead of the green bean one because green beans were out of season and looking terribly limp at the grocer. You know how they get those brown spots and go all rubbery? You just can't serve that to guests. Anyway, Joanne wouldn't speak to me for the rest of the meal. She just sat there chewing her turkey—which was terribly dry, by the way, she never bastes it enough. You have to baste a turkey every forty-five minutes or it turns to sawdust in your mouth. I use a little chicken broth and melted butter for mine. I used to have this wonderful baster with a little flavor injector needle, but the bulb cracked. They just don't make rubber like they used to. Everything cracks or melts in the dishwasher now. I bought some plastic storage containers last month and they warped on the top rack! The lids don't snap on anymore, so now I have to use aluminum foil, which is so wasteful. But no... no, I'm not wrong about this. I know exactly what they're doing with the committee.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would be able to successfully erase your contributions to the family from everyone's memory?\n\nPatient: Erase my contributions? Yes, absolutely. That's the whole point of their plan. I came in here because it finally dawned on me—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, they were actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd I've kept this family together, let me tell you. When my father-in-law passed, I was the one who sorted through all his things in the attic. Dust everywhere. I found these beautiful old glass Christmas ornaments, the fragile kind from Germany. They were wrapped in newspaper from 1964. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now all the ornaments at the store are made of cheap plastic and covered in glitter that gets all over the rug. I vacuumed for an hour last Christmas just trying to get the glitter out of the fibers. I have one of those vacuums with the clear canister so you can see all the dirt spinning around. It's very satisfying, really, but it's so heavy to drag up the stairs. My knees aren't what they used to be. The doctor told me to take glucosamine, but the pills are the size of horse tranquilizers. I have to break them in half just to swallow them without choking. I choked on a piece of hard candy once when I was ten, a butterscotch disc. Terrifying. My uncle had to do the Heimlich maneuver on me... but yes, they are definitely trying to erase me. They want it to be like I was never here at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there could be any other explanation for the parish committee's actions that doesn't involve a plot against you?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? No, no, there isn't one. It's clear as day to me now. I mean, I told you, I came in because I finally realized it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, like normal families do. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd it's not just the church things, either. It's the little things. Like the way they look at me when I bring my famous macaroni salad to the summer picnics. I use the little shell pasta, not the elbows. The elbows get too mushy if you leave them in the dressing too long. You have to use a good quality mayonnaise, too, none of that low-fat nonsense. It separates in the heat. We had a picnic at the lake back in 2005, and it was so hot, I swear the pavement was melting. My husband—God rest his soul—got a terrible sunburn on his shoulders because he refused to wear the sunscreen I bought. It was that spray kind. I don't trust the sprays, honestly. You never know if you're getting an even coat. I always end up with these strange streaks of red and white, like a candy cane. Peppermint is my favorite flavor of tea, by the way. It settles the stomach. I have a cup every night before bed while I watch the evening news. The news is just so depressing lately, though, isn't it? All those terrible things happening in the world. But yes, there's no other explanation. They are plotting against me, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is out to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Harm me personally? Well, yes, in a way. I mean, it's like I've been saying, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. It's a spiritual harm, an erasure. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, they changed the linens last week. They used to use the beautiful Irish linen with the hand-embroidered crosses on the edges. My grandmother taught me how to embroider when I was a little girl. We would sit on the porch in the summer, and she had this big wooden hoop. I pricked my finger so many times on those tiny needles. You have to be so careful with blood on white fabric. Cold water and a little bit of salt, that's the trick. Never use hot water, it sets the stain right in. I ruined a perfectly good silk blouse that way once. I wore it to a wedding, my cousin Linda's wedding. She married a man who sold insurance, very boring fellow, but he had a nice smile. They had a chocolate fountain at the reception. Oh, what a mess that was! People were dropping strawberries into it and splashing chocolate all over the tablecloths. I just don't understand the appeal of those things. It seems terribly unsanitary, everyone dipping their sticks into the same flow of chocolate. But yes, pushing me out, erasing me... that's exactly the harm they are doing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your in-laws using the parish committee against you might be an exaggeration or misunderstanding of their actual actions?\n\nPatient: An exaggeration? No, I... I don't think you're hearing me. I wouldn't have come in here if it was just a misunderstanding. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. This isn't a petty squabble. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nThey think they're so clever, whispering after mass. Mass used to be so peaceful. Father Thomas had such a soothing voice, almost like a radio announcer. He used to do the late-night jazz hour on the college station before he went to seminary, you know. Jazz is too unpredictable for me, though. All those horns blaring out of nowhere. I prefer classical music. Mozart, Beethoven. My mother played the piano beautifully. She had this upright Baldwin in the parlor, polished mahogany. I tried to learn, but my fingers were always too stiff. The piano teacher, Mrs. Gable, she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint. Peppermint again, see? It really is everywhere. But she was very strict, used a metronome that ticked so loudly it gave me a headache. Tick, tick, tick... just like a clock counting down. And that's what they're doing, counting down the days until they can just scrub me right out of the family history. No, it's not an exaggeration at all. It's exactly what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your feelings of being pushed out by your in-laws are not based on real events?\n\nPatient: Not based on real events? Doctor, I feel like a broken record, but I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, the way families normally do. It is very real. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me completely out and make every single person forget what I have done for this family! \n\nReal events! I organized every family communion and baptism for thirty years! I made the little favor bags for the tables by hand. I used a lovely blue organza fabric for my nephew's christening. Organza is so tricky to sew, you know. It frays if you even look at it wrong. You really have to use pinking shears. My mother had a pair of heavy metal pinking shears that weighed a ton. She kept them in one of those Danish butter cookie tins. You know the ones, the dark blue tins? It is always such a terrible disappointment when you open one hoping for a cookie and it is just full of spools of thread and needles. I love those little pretzel-shaped cookies with the large sugar crystals on top. \n\nAlthough, sugar is getting so terribly expensive these days. I saw the price at the grocery store on Tuesday and nearly had a heart attack. I always do my shopping on Tuesdays because the store is quieter. The weekends are just a madhouse, people bumping into your cart and not even apologizing. Manners have just completely gone out the window in this country. People don't even hold the door for you anymore. But no, I am not making this up. The things they are doing at the church to get rid of me are very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your in-laws' actions are not actually intended to harm or erase you?\n\nPatient: Not intended to harm me? How can you even suggest that? I keep telling you, I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me, they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. It's completely deliberate! \n\nThey know exactly what they're doing. Just like they know I always bring the potato salad to the summer picnic, but last year my sister-in-law brought one too. Hers had celery in it. Who puts celery in potato salad? It completely ruins the texture. You need a nice, soft bite, maybe a little sweet relish. My mother used to make her own relish from the cucumbers in our garden. We had such a big garden when I was growing up. Tomatoes, zucchini, green beans... the green beans would climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the garage. We had to paint that garage every three years because the paint would peel from the humidity. It was a pale yellow color, sort of like butter. Butter makes everything better, doesn't it? I always put a pat of butter on my oatmeal in the morning. It melts in so nicely. But yes, their intentions are clear as day. They want me gone and forgotten.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Did I do something? Well... I mean, yes, I suppose I must have. I think about it constantly. I keep thinking I just ruined everything for my family and the parish. It's this terrible, heavy feeling. And sometimes I feel like even the little things that go wrong in my day are my punishment for it. 

Like on Sunday, I dropped a whole jar of strawberry preserves on the kitchen floor. It shattered everywhere, sticky glass all over the linoleum. It felt like a punishment, just another mess I made. Linoleum is so terribly hard to keep clean once something sticky gets on it. You really have to get down on your hands and knees with hot water and vinegar. Vinegar is a miracle worker, you know. My grandmother used to swear by it for cleaning the windows. She'd use old newspapers instead of paper towels. She said paper towels left streaks. Newspapers used to be so thick, full of real news, but now they're so thin and flimsy. My father would sit in his armchair every evening and read the evening edition from front to back. He smoked a pipe, Cherry Blend tobacco. The smell was so sweet, it would settle into the upholstery. You don't see people smoking pipes much anymore. It's all those electronic things now, blowing out clouds of vapor that smell like cotton candy. It’s very unnatural. Cotton candy always made my fingers too sticky at the county fair anyway. I preferred the caramel apples. But yes, the preserves on the floor... I just sat there and cried, because I know I've ruined everything with the family.

My nephew David was looking for a job, but he only wants to work with computers. You go through so many batteries, and they're so expensive now. I went to the pharmacy to get some last week, and they had them locked up behind this plastic case! You have to press a button and wait for someone to come unlock it. I stood there for twenty minutes. The girl who finally came over looked like she was barely out of middle school. She had bright pink hair, just bright neon pink, right down to her shoulders. I don't understand how jobs let people look like that nowadays. When I worked the checkout at the grocery store—before they put in all those self-checkout machines where you have to scan your own apples—we had a strict dress code. Well, yes, of course it's about me! It couldn't possibly be about anything else. I told you, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee specifically to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI've done so much, you know. I organized the church bake sale for fifteen years straight. Fifteen years! I used to make these lemon bars from scratch. Not from a box, mind you. You have to use fresh lemons, Meyer lemons if you can find them, and you grate the zest right into the sugar before you mix the batter. It releases the oils, makes it so much more flavorful. My mother taught me that. She had a lemon tree in her backyard when I was growing up in California. Oh, the weather there was just perfect. Never too humid. I can't stand the humidity here, it makes my joints ache so terribly. I bought this special copper bracelet that's supposed to help with the arthritis, but I think it's turning my wrist a little green. I read in a magazine in the dentist's waiting room that you can paint clear nail polish on the inside of the jewelry to stop that from happening. The dentist was running forty-five minutes late that day, too. Just left me sitting there flipping through old copies of Better Homes and Gardens... but yes, the altar cloths, the committee, it's all about me. They just want me gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of your in-laws' actions and the parish committee's decisions might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No, no, I don't think it's incorrect at all. I know what I see. I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nMy husband's family has always been like this, you know, holding grudges over the silliest things. Like at Thanksgiving back in '98, I brought a sweet potato casserole instead of the green bean one because green beans were out of season and looking terribly limp at the grocer. You know how they get those brown spots and go all rubbery? You just can't serve that to guests. Anyway, Joanne wouldn't speak to me for the rest of the meal. She just sat there chewing her turkey—which was terribly dry, by the way, she never bastes it enough. You have to baste a turkey every forty-five minutes or it turns to sawdust in your mouth. I use a little chicken broth and melted butter for mine. I used to have this wonderful baster with a little flavor injector needle, but the bulb cracked. They just don't make rubber like they used to. Everything cracks or melts in the dishwasher now. I bought some plastic storage containers last month and they warped on the top rack! The lids don't snap on anymore, so now I have to use aluminum foil, which is so wasteful. But no... no, I'm not wrong about this. I know exactly what they're doing with the committee.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would be able to successfully erase your contributions to the family from everyone's memory?\n\nPatient: Erase my contributions? Yes, absolutely. That's the whole point of their plan. I came in here because it finally dawned on me—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, they were actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd I've kept this family together, let me tell you. When my father-in-law passed, I was the one who sorted through all his things in the attic. Dust everywhere. I found these beautiful old glass Christmas ornaments, the fragile kind from Germany. They were wrapped in newspaper from 1964. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now all the ornaments at the store are made of cheap plastic and covered in glitter that gets all over the rug. I vacuumed for an hour last Christmas just trying to get the glitter out of the fibers. I have one of those vacuums with the clear canister so you can see all the dirt spinning around. It's very satisfying, really, but it's so heavy to drag up the stairs. My knees aren't what they used to be. The doctor told me to take glucosamine, but the pills are the size of horse tranquilizers. I have to break them in half just to swallow them without choking. I choked on a piece of hard candy once when I was ten, a butterscotch disc. Terrifying. My uncle had to do the Heimlich maneuver on me... but yes, they are definitely trying to erase me. They want it to be like I was never here at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there could be any other explanation for the parish committee's actions that doesn't involve a plot against you?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? No, no, there isn't one. It's clear as day to me now. I mean, I told you, I came in because I finally realized it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, like normal families do. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd it's not just the church things, either. It's the little things. Like the way they look at me when I bring my famous macaroni salad to the summer picnics. I use the little shell pasta, not the elbows. The elbows get too mushy if you leave them in the dressing too long. You have to use a good quality mayonnaise, too, none of that low-fat nonsense. It separates in the heat. We had a picnic at the lake back in 2005, and it was so hot, I swear the pavement was melting. My husband—God rest his soul—got a terrible sunburn on his shoulders because he refused to wear the sunscreen I bought. It was that spray kind. I don't trust the sprays, honestly. You never know if you're getting an even coat. I always end up with these strange streaks of red and white, like a candy cane. Peppermint is my favorite flavor of tea, by the way. It settles the stomach. I have a cup every night before bed while I watch the evening news. The news is just so depressing lately, though, isn't it? All those terrible things happening in the world. But yes, there's no other explanation. They are plotting against me, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is out to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Harm me personally? Well, yes, in a way. I mean, it's like I've been saying, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. It's a spiritual harm, an erasure. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, they changed the linens last week. They used to use the beautiful Irish linen with the hand-embroidered crosses on the edges. My grandmother taught me how to embroider when I was a little girl. We would sit on the porch in the summer, and she had this big wooden hoop. I pricked my finger so many times on those tiny needles. You have to be so careful with blood on white fabric. Cold water and a little bit of salt, that's the trick. Never use hot water, it sets the stain right in. I ruined a perfectly good silk blouse that way once. I wore it to a wedding, my cousin Linda's wedding. She married a man who sold insurance, very boring fellow, but he had a nice smile. They had a chocolate fountain at the reception. Oh, what a mess that was! People were dropping strawberries into it and splashing chocolate all over the tablecloths. I just don't understand the appeal of those things. It seems terribly unsanitary, everyone dipping their sticks into the same flow of chocolate. But yes, pushing me out, erasing me... that's exactly the harm they are doing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your in-laws using the parish committee against you might be an exaggeration or misunderstanding of their actual actions?\n\nPatient: An exaggeration? No, I... I don't think you're hearing me. I wouldn't have come in here if it was just a misunderstanding. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. This isn't a petty squabble. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nThey think they're so clever, whispering after mass. Mass used to be so peaceful. Father Thomas had such a soothing voice, almost like a radio announcer. He used to do the late-night jazz hour on the college station before he went to seminary, you know. Jazz is too unpredictable for me, though. All those horns blaring out of nowhere. I prefer classical music. Mozart, Beethoven. My mother played the piano beautifully. She had this upright Baldwin in the parlor, polished mahogany. I tried to learn, but my fingers were always too stiff. The piano teacher, Mrs. Gable, she always smelled like mothballs and peppermint. Peppermint again, see? It really is everywhere. But she was very strict, used a metronome that ticked so loudly it gave me a headache. Tick, tick, tick... just like a clock counting down. And that's what they're doing, counting down the days until they can just scrub me right out of the family history. No, it's not an exaggeration at all. It's exactly what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your feelings of being pushed out by your in-laws are not based on real events?\n\nPatient: Not based on real events? Doctor, I feel like a broken record, but I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, the way families normally do. It is very real. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me completely out and make every single person forget what I have done for this family! \n\nReal events! I organized every family communion and baptism for thirty years! I made the little favor bags for the tables by hand. I used a lovely blue organza fabric for my nephew's christening. Organza is so tricky to sew, you know. It frays if you even look at it wrong. You really have to use pinking shears. My mother had a pair of heavy metal pinking shears that weighed a ton. She kept them in one of those Danish butter cookie tins. You know the ones, the dark blue tins? It is always such a terrible disappointment when you open one hoping for a cookie and it is just full of spools of thread and needles. I love those little pretzel-shaped cookies with the large sugar crystals on top. \n\nAlthough, sugar is getting so terribly expensive these days. I saw the price at the grocery store on Tuesday and nearly had a heart attack. I always do my shopping on Tuesdays because the store is quieter. The weekends are just a madhouse, people bumping into your cart and not even apologizing. Manners have just completely gone out the window in this country. People don't even hold the door for you anymore. But no, I am not making this up. The things they are doing at the church to get rid of me are very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your in-laws' actions are not actually intended to harm or erase you?\n\nPatient: Not intended to harm me? How can you even suggest that? I keep telling you, I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me, they were using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. It's completely deliberate! \n\nThey know exactly what they're doing. Just like they know I always bring the potato salad to the summer picnic, but last year my sister-in-law brought one too. Hers had celery in it. Who puts celery in potato salad? It completely ruins the texture. You need a nice, soft bite, maybe a little sweet relish. My mother used to make her own relish from the cucumbers in our garden. We had such a big garden when I was growing up. Tomatoes, zucchini, green beans... the green beans would climb all the way up the trellis on the side of the garage. We had to paint that garage every three years because the paint would peel from the humidity. It was a pale yellow color, sort of like butter. Butter makes everything better, doesn't it? I always put a pat of butter on my oatmeal in the morning. It melts in so nicely. But yes, their intentions are clear as day. They want me gone and forgotten.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might have done something to unintentionally contribute to your in-laws' behavior towards you?\n\nPatient: Did I do something? Well... I mean, yes, I suppose I must have. I think about it constantly. I keep thinking I just ruined everything for my family and the parish. It's this terrible, heavy feeling. And sometimes I feel like even the little things that go wrong in my day are my punishment for it. \n\nLike on Sunday, I dropped a whole jar of strawberry preserves on the kitchen floor. It shattered everywhere, sticky glass all over the linoleum. It felt like a punishment, just another mess I made. Linoleum is so terribly hard to keep clean once something sticky gets on it. You really have to get down on your hands and knees with hot water and vinegar. Vinegar is a miracle worker, you know. My grandmother used to swear by it for cleaning the windows. She'd use old newspapers instead of paper towels. She said paper towels left streaks. Newspapers used to be so thick, full of real news, but now they're so thin and flimsy. My father would sit in his armchair every evening and read the evening edition from front to back. He smoked a pipe, Cherry Blend tobacco. The smell was so sweet, it would settle into the upholstery. You don't see people smoking pipes much anymore. It's all those electronic things now, blowing out clouds of vapor that smell like cotton candy. It’s very unnatural. Cotton candy always made my fingers too sticky at the county fair anyway. I preferred the caramel apples. But yes, the preserves on the floor... I just sat there and cried, because I know I've ruined everything with the family.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Another reason? No, doctor, there isn't another reason. I know I keep repeating myself, but it's the absolute truth—my in-laws are using the parish committee and the altar itself to completely push me out. They are orchestrating all of it just to make everyone forget everything I have done for this family! Changing the cloths to green was a deliberate slap in the face. 

Green! And completely out of the liturgical season, too. Usually, you only see that specific shade of green around St. Patrick's Day. We used to go all out for St. Patrick's Day when my husband was still working. I'd make a huge brisket, corned beef and cabbage. You have to boil the cabbage just right or it turns into absolute mush, and nobody wants mushy cabbage. And the smell! Oh, it lingers in the house for days. You really have to open all the windows to air it out properly. 

I do love opening the windows in early spring, though. The breeze catches the curtains... I made these lovely sheer curtains for the living room window a few years back. They let just the right amount of light in. Sunlight is so terribly important for houseplants. My African violets just thrive in that front window. You have to be so careful watering them, though. You have to water them from the bottom, in the little saucer, because if water gets on the fuzzy leaves, they rot. It's a very delicate balance. You can't just pour water over everything and expect it to survive. But no, there is no other reason for the green cloths. It's just them, trying to erase me.

My nephew David was looking for a job, but he only wants to work with computers. Well, anyone paying attention would! That's exactly why I came in here today, to tell someone what's really happening. Because I finally realized they are not just arguing with me. It's not just normal family bickering. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI was just trying to explain this to my neighbor, Betty. She came over to borrow my garden shears because her hydrangeas are completely out of control. You have to prune them back in late winter, you know, or they just take over the whole front walkway. I told her she needs to use aluminum sulfate if she wants the blooms to stay blue, because the soil around here is much too alkaline. My husband used to test the dirt every spring with those little paper strips. He bought a whole box of them at the local hardware store before it closed down and turned into that awful giant warehouse place out on the highway. I can't stand going in there. The ceilings are too high and those fluorescent lights just buzz and buzz, it gives me a terrible migraine. And you can walk down ten aisles and never see a single employee to ask where the furnace filters are... but yes, Betty would agree if she just looked at the facts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is following you or watching you?\n\nPatient: Following me? Well, I don't know about following me down the street, but they are definitely watching me. My in-laws, I mean. They have to be watching me to know exactly how to use the altar and the parish committee to push me out. They are coordinating the whole thing just to make everyone forget what I have done for this family, and they couldn't do that if they weren't keeping tabs on me.\n\nI used to keep tabs on the neighborhood watch, you know. Back when we had a proper neighborhood watch. We'd take turns walking the block with flashlights. We had these heavy metal flashlights, the kind that take those big D batteries. You go through so many batteries, and they're so expensive now. I went to the pharmacy to get some last week, and they had them locked up behind this plastic case! You have to press a button and wait for someone to come unlock it. I stood there for twenty minutes. The girl who finally came over looked like she was barely out of middle school. She had bright pink hair, just bright neon pink, right down to her shoulders. I don't understand how jobs let people look like that nowadays. When I worked the checkout at the grocery store—before they put in all those self-checkout machines where you have to scan your own apples—we had a strict dress code. White collared shirts, navy skirts. Very professional. But anyway... yes, they are watching me, making sure their little plot with the committee works.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would actually be able to control the parish committee and the altar in the way you've described?\n\nPatient: Control the committee? Of course they can! That's exactly what I've been trying to tell you. I came in here specifically because I finally realized it wasn't just normal arguing anymore. They are absolutely using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and erase every single thing I've done for this family. Joanne has everyone on that committee wrapped around her little finger. \n\nShe always did know how to talk people into things. Like that time she convinced my brother to buy that awful timeshare in Florida. It was somewhere near Orlando, I think, but not near the parks. They had to sit through a three-hour presentation just to get free breakfast coupons. I told him it was a scam, but he wouldn't listen. He never listens. He bought a used car once from a man in a parking lot, just handed over cash! The transmission blew out two weeks later on the interstate. They had to get it towed all the way back to town, and you know how much tow trucks charge these days. It's highway robbery. The driver was a nice young man, though, helped him get his golf clubs out of the trunk. My brother loves golf, but he has a terrible swing. He slices it every time... but yes, Joanne is controlling the whole committee, there's no doubt in my mind about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the changes in the altar cloths or the parish committee's actions are specifically about you, or could they be about something else entirely?\n\nPatient: About me? Well, yes, of course it's about me! It couldn't possibly be about anything else. I told you, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee specifically to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nI've done so much, you know. I organized the church bake sale for fifteen years straight. Fifteen years! I used to make these lemon bars from scratch. Not from a box, mind you. You have to use fresh lemons, Meyer lemons if you can find them, and you grate the zest right into the sugar before you mix the batter. It releases the oils, makes it so much more flavorful. My mother taught me that. She had a lemon tree in her backyard when I was growing up in California. Oh, the weather there was just perfect. Never too humid. I can't stand the humidity here, it makes my joints ache so terribly. I bought this special copper bracelet that's supposed to help with the arthritis, but I think it's turning my wrist a little green. I read in a magazine in the dentist's waiting room that you can paint clear nail polish on the inside of the jewelry to stop that from happening. The dentist was running forty-five minutes late that day, too. Just left me sitting there flipping through old copies of Better Homes and Gardens... but yes, the altar cloths, the committee, it's all about me. They just want me gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your interpretation of your in-laws' actions and the parish committee's decisions might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: Incorrect? No, no, I don't think it's incorrect at all. I know what I see. I came in here because I finally saw the truth of it—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone forget what I have done for this family. \n\nMy husband's family has always been like this, you know, holding grudges over the silliest things. Like at Thanksgiving back in '98, I brought a sweet potato casserole instead of the green bean one because green beans were out of season and looking terribly limp at the grocer. You know how they get those brown spots and go all rubbery? You just can't serve that to guests. Anyway, Joanne wouldn't speak to me for the rest of the meal. She just sat there chewing her turkey—which was terribly dry, by the way, she never bastes it enough. You have to baste a turkey every forty-five minutes or it turns to sawdust in your mouth. I use a little chicken broth and melted butter for mine. I used to have this wonderful baster with a little flavor injector needle, but the bulb cracked. They just don't make rubber like they used to. Everything cracks or melts in the dishwasher now. I bought some plastic storage containers last month and they warped on the top rack! The lids don't snap on anymore, so now I have to use aluminum foil, which is so wasteful. But no... no, I'm not wrong about this. I know exactly what they're doing with the committee.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your in-laws would be able to successfully erase your contributions to the family from everyone's memory?\n\nPatient: Erase my contributions? Yes, absolutely. That's the whole point of their plan. I came in here because it finally dawned on me—I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, they were actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd I've kept this family together, let me tell you. When my father-in-law passed, I was the one who sorted through all his things in the attic. Dust everywhere. I found these beautiful old glass Christmas ornaments, the fragile kind from Germany. They were wrapped in newspaper from 1964. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now all the ornaments at the store are made of cheap plastic and covered in glitter that gets all over the rug. I vacuumed for an hour last Christmas just trying to get the glitter out of the fibers. I have one of those vacuums with the clear canister so you can see all the dirt spinning around. It's very satisfying, really, but it's so heavy to drag up the stairs. My knees aren't what they used to be. The doctor told me to take glucosamine, but the pills are the size of horse tranquilizers. I have to break them in half just to swallow them without choking. I choked on a piece of hard candy once when I was ten, a butterscotch disc. Terrifying. My uncle had to do the Heimlich maneuver on me... but yes, they are definitely trying to erase me. They want it to be like I was never here at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there could be any other explanation for the parish committee's actions that doesn't involve a plot against you?\n\nPatient: Another explanation? No, no, there isn't one. It's clear as day to me now. I mean, I told you, I came in because I finally realized it. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore, like normal families do. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nAnd it's not just the church things, either. It's the little things. Like the way they look at me when I bring my famous macaroni salad to the summer picnics. I use the little shell pasta, not the elbows. The elbows get too mushy if you leave them in the dressing too long. You have to use a good quality mayonnaise, too, none of that low-fat nonsense. It separates in the heat. We had a picnic at the lake back in 2005, and it was so hot, I swear the pavement was melting. My husband—God rest his soul—got a terrible sunburn on his shoulders because he refused to wear the sunscreen I bought. It was that spray kind. I don't trust the sprays, honestly. You never know if you're getting an even coat. I always end up with these strange streaks of red and white, like a candy cane. Peppermint is my favorite flavor of tea, by the way. It settles the stomach. I have a cup every night before bed while I watch the evening news. The news is just so depressing lately, though, isn't it? All those terrible things happening in the world. But yes, there's no other explanation. They are plotting against me, plain and simple.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone is out to harm you personally?\n\nPatient: Harm me personally? Well, yes, in a way. I mean, it's like I've been saying, I came in here because I finally put the pieces together. I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. They are using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. It's a spiritual harm, an erasure. \n\nSpeaking of the altar, they changed the linens last week. They used to use the beautiful Irish linen with the hand-embroidered crosses on the edges. My grandmother taught me how to embroider when I was a little girl. We would sit on the porch in the summer, and she had this big wooden hoop. I pricked my finger so many times on those tiny needles. You have to be so careful with blood on white fabric. Cold water and a little bit of salt, that's the trick. Never use hot water, it sets the stain right in. I ruined a perfectly good silk blouse that way once. I wore it to a wedding, my cousin Linda's wedding. She married a man who sold insurance, very boring fellow, but he had a nice smile. They had a chocolate fountain at the reception. Oh, what a mess that was! People were dropping strawberries into it and splashing chocolate all over the tablecloths. I just don't understand the appeal of those things. It seems terribly unsanitary, everyone dipping their sticks into the same flow of chocolate. But yes, pushing me out, erasing me... that's exactly the harm they are doing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your in-laws using the parish committee against you might be an exaggeration or misunderstanding of their actual actions?\n\nPatient: An exaggeration? No, I... I don't think you're hearing me. I wouldn't have come in here if it was just a misunderstanding. I came in because I knew my in-laws were not just arguing with me anymore. This isn't a petty squabble. They are actively using the altar and the parish committee to push me out and make everyone completely forget what I have done for this family. \n\nThey think they're so clever, whispering after mass. Mass used to be so peaceful. Father Thomas had such a soothing voice, almost like a radio announcer. He used to do the late-night jazz hour on the college station before he went to seminary, you know. Jazz is too unpredictable for me, though. All those horns blaring out of nowhere. I prefer classical music. Mozart, Beethoven. My mother played the piano beautifully. She had this upright Baldwin in the parlor, polished mahogany.
